===== S01 subgoal =====
--- prompt ---
Given this segment of a lecture transcript, what goal do you think the lecturer is trying to achieve in this segment? Name the goal starting with a verb. Be fine-grained.

Write the goal for a learner, so that when they look back at it they immediately remember what was covered.
- First work out what this part of the lecture mainly does, for example: raises questions, looks at everyday examples, defines terms, compares things, explains why something matters, or previews the course. The goal must describe that main activity.
- If the part mainly previews the course or recaps earlier material, the goal is about that preview or recap (e.g. "See what this course covers: its aims, main topics and hands-on parts"), not about the individual topics it lists.
- Do not name topics that are only mentioned in passing.
- Start with a simple verb such as Learn, Understand, See, Compare, Explore or Find out.
- Name the concrete topics, terms or examples covered, e.g. "Learn the differences between viruses and bacteria".
- Use plain, everyday words. Avoid vague or academic words such as delineate, contextualize, establish, foundational, framework, paradigm.
- Keep it short: one line, at most 15 words.

Reply with only the goal.

Segment:
Hello, welcome to the NPTEL MOOCs course on Foundation for Virtual and Augmented Reality Systems. We are going to start lecture number 1, which is on introductory concepts. I am Dr. Samit Bhattacharya, a member of the Department of Computer Science and Engineering, IIT Guwahati.
--- thinking ---
Thinking Process:

1.  **Analyze the Segment:** The segment is a greeting and introduction.
    *   "Hello, welcome to the NPTEL MOOCs course on Foundation for Virtual and Augmented Reality Systems." (Identifies the course and platform).
    *   "We are going to start lecture number 1, which is on introductory concepts." (Sets the stage for the content).
    *   "I am Dr. Samit Bhattacharya, a member of the Department of Computer Science and Engineering, IIT Guwahati." (Introduces the lecturer).

2.  **Determine the Main Activity/Goal:** The lecturer is introducing the course, the topic (introductory concepts), and themselves. This is a classic course introduction/preview.

3.  **Apply Constraints:**
    *   Start with a simple verb (Learn, Understand, See, etc.).
    *   Be fine-grained and concrete.
    *   Focus on the main activity (preview/introduction).
    *   Keep it short (max 15 words).
    *   Use plain language.

4.  **Drafting Potential Goals (Internal Check):**
    *   *Draft 1:* Learn about the course on Virtual and Augmented Reality Systems. (A bit too general).
    *   *Draft 2:* See the course introduction and meet the lecturer for VR/AR Systems. (Good, but maybe slightly clunky).
    *   *Draft 3 (Focusing on the core action):* Understand the course structure and meet the instructor for VR/AR Systems. (Accurate and concise).

5.  **Final Selection and Refinement:** The segment primarily sets the context for the entire course.

6.  **Final Output Generation.**
--- answer ---
See what the course on Virtual and Augmented Reality Systems covers and meet the instructor.

===== S02 subgoal =====
--- prompt ---
Given this segment of a lecture transcript, what goal do you think the lecturer is trying to achieve in this segment? Name the goal starting with a verb. Be fine-grained.

Write the goal for a learner, so that when they look back at it they immediately remember what was covered.
- First work out what this part of the lecture mainly does, for example: raises questions, looks at everyday examples, defines terms, compares things, explains why something matters, or previews the course. The goal must describe that main activity.
- If the part mainly previews the course or recaps earlier material, the goal is about that preview or recap (e.g. "See what this course covers: its aims, main topics and hands-on parts"), not about the individual topics it lists.
- Do not name topics that are only mentioned in passing.
- Start with a simple verb such as Learn, Understand, See, Compare, Explore or Find out.
- Name the concrete topics, terms or examples covered, e.g. "Learn the differences between viruses and bacteria".
- Use plain, everyday words. Avoid vague or academic words such as delineate, contextualize, establish, foundational, framework, paradigm.
- Keep it short: one line, at most 15 words.

Reply with only the goal.

Segment:
I will be the instructor for this course, and with that, let me start the lecture. So, as you noticed, the title of the course talks about two terms: virtual reality and augmented reality. Now, in brief, these are often called VR and AR. Now, the thing is, nowadays we are hearing a few similar terms. What are those terms? Many of you might have heard of these terms.
--- thinking ---
Thinking Process:

1.  **Analyze the Segment:**
    *   The lecturer introduces themselves and the course.
    *   The course title mentions "virtual reality" and "augmented reality."
    *   The lecturer states these are often called "VR and AR."
    *   The lecturer then notes that "we are hearing a few similar terms."
    *   The lecturer asks the audience what those terms are, implying they are about to be discussed or introduced.

2.  **Determine the Main Activity/Goal:**
    *   The segment is primarily introducing the core concepts (VR and AR) and setting up the scope of the discussion by mentioning related, similar terms.
    *   It is defining the scope and introducing key terminology.

3.  **Apply Constraints & Refine the Goal:**
    *   *Start with a simple verb:* Learn, Understand, See, etc.
    *   *Be fine-grained and concrete:* Must mention VR and AR.
    *   *Keep it short (max 15 words):*
    *   *Avoid jargon:* Use plain language.

4.  **Drafting Options:**
    *   *Draft 1 (Focus on definition):* Learn the basic definitions of virtual reality (VR) and augmented reality (AR). (A bit too narrow, misses the "similar terms" setup).
    *   *Draft 2 (Focus on scope):* Understand the main terms of the course, including VR and AR, and related concepts. (Good, but maybe slightly vague).
    *   *Draft 3 (Combining introduction and terms):* Learn the core terms of the course: virtual reality (VR) and augmented reality (AR). (Concise and accurate to the immediate content).

5.  **Final Selection (Checking against constraints):**
    *   Goal: Learn the core terms of the course: virtual reality (VR) and augmented reality (AR).
    *   Starts with a simple verb? Yes (Learn).
    *   Fine-grained? Yes (VR and AR).
    *   Short (max 15 words)? Yes (12 words).
    *   Plain language? Yes.

6.  **Final Output Generation.**
--- answer ---
Learn the core terms of the course: virtual reality (VR) and augmented reality (AR).

===== S03 subgoal =====
--- prompt ---
Given this segment of a lecture transcript, what goal do you think the lecturer is trying to achieve in this segment? Name the goal starting with a verb. Be fine-grained.

Write the goal for a learner, so that when they look back at it they immediately remember what was covered.
- First work out what this part of the lecture mainly does, for example: raises questions, looks at everyday examples, defines terms, compares things, explains why something matters, or previews the course. The goal must describe that main activity.
- If the part mainly previews the course or recaps earlier material, the goal is about that preview or recap (e.g. "See what this course covers: its aims, main topics and hands-on parts"), not about the individual topics it lists.
- Do not name topics that are only mentioned in passing.
- Start with a simple verb such as Learn, Understand, See, Compare, Explore or Find out.
- Name the concrete topics, terms or examples covered, e.g. "Learn the differences between viruses and bacteria".
- Use plain, everyday words. Avoid vague or academic words such as delineate, contextualize, establish, foundational, framework, paradigm.
- Keep it short: one line, at most 15 words.

Reply with only the goal.

Segment:
First of all, we are often hearing the term XR, which stands for extended reality. Then we have heard, of course, of VR, which stands for virtual reality, which is part of the course title. We also have heard of AR, augmented reality, another part of the course title. Along with that, we are also hearing nowadays another term called MR, mixed reality.
--- thinking ---
Thinking Process:

1.  **Analyze the Segment:** The lecturer introduces and defines several related terms: XR (Extended Reality), VR (Virtual Reality), AR (Augmented Reality), and MR (Mixed Reality).
2.  **Determine the Main Activity:** The primary action is defining and listing key terminology related to the subject matter (reality technologies).
3.  **Identify the Goal Type (Fine-grained, Learner-focused):** The goal must tell the learner *what* they learned in this specific segment.
4.  **Apply Constraints:**
    *   Start with a simple verb (Learn, Understand, See, etc.).
    *   Be fine-grained and concrete (name the terms).
    *   Use plain language.
    *   Keep it short (max 15 words).
    *   The goal must describe the main activity (defining terms).
5.  **Drafting the Goal:**
    *   *Attempt 1:* Learn the definitions of XR, VR, AR, and MR. (A bit dry, but accurate.)
    *   *Attempt 2 (Focusing on the relationship):* Understand the different types of reality: XR, VR, AR, and MR. (Better, more active.)
    *   *Attempt 3 (Refining for clarity and conciseness):* Learn the key terms: Extended, Virtual, Augmented, and Mixed Reality. (Very clear and covers the core content.)

6.  **Final Selection Check:**
    *   Starts with a simple verb? Yes (Learn).
    *   Fine-grained/Concrete? Yes (XR, VR, AR, MR).
    *   Plain language? Yes.
    *   Short (max 15 words)? Yes (10 words).

7.  **Final Output Generation.**
--- answer ---
Learn the key terms: Extended, Virtual, Augmented, and Mixed Reality.

- Name the concrete topics, terms or examples covered, e.g. "Learn the differences between viruses and bacteria".

Segment:
What do these terms refer to? What are they all about? So, when we hear these terms, naturally, a few questions come to our mind.
--- thinking ---
Thinking Process:

1.  **Analyze the Segment:** The segment is: "What do these terms refer to? What are they all about? So, when we hear these terms, naturally, a few questions come to our mind."
2.  **Determine the Main Activity:** The lecturer is posing questions about "these terms" and asking what they mean or what they are about. This is an introductory move designed to prompt the audience to think about the concepts that are about to be discussed or have just been mentioned (though the context suggests they are about to be defined).     *   *Attempt 2 (Focus on definition):* Understand what the key terms in this topic refer to. (Better, but "key terms" is slightly vague).
    *   *Attempt 3 (Focus on the action):* Explore the meaning and scope of the terms being introduced. (Good, but maybe too academic).

6.  **Final Selection:** Understand what the specific terms in this lecture are about. (This accurately reflects the function of the segment: prompting the audience to consider the meaning of the terms.)
--- answer ---
Understand what the specific terms in this lecture are about.

- Name the concrete topics, terms or examples covered, e.g. "Learn the differences between viruses and bacteria".
- Use plain, everyday words. Avoid vague or academic words such as delineate, contextualize, establish, foundational, framework, paradigm.

Reply with only the goal.

Segment:

--- thinking ---
Thinking Process:

1.  **Analyze the Segment:** The segment is: "What are their meanings? Then, do they represent the same technology? So, we have mentioned multiple terms. Are they the same? Do they represent the same technology?"
2.  **Determine the Main Activity:** The lecturer is explicitly pointing out that multiple terms have been used and is raising questions about the definitions and equivalency of these terms. The core activity is clarifying terminology and addressing potential confusion.
3.  **Identify the Goal Type (Fine-grained, Learner-focused):** The goal must tell the learner *what* they will gain from this segment.
4.  **Draft Potential Goals (Focusing on the action):**
    *   Clarify the meanings of the terms used.
    *   Determine if the different terms refer to the same technology.
    *   Compare and contrast the various terms mentioned.

    *   *Draft 2 (More direct):* Compare different terms to see if they describe the same technology. (11 words)

Reply with only the goal.

Segment:
What is that technology? Or alternatively, do these multiple terms represent or refer to different technologies? In that case, how are they different from each other? One interesting question that may come to your mind along with the previous ones is the significance. So, I am talking about this term, even this course itself, Foundation of Virtual and Augmented Reality Systems. You might be wondering, why so much interest? Why should I learn this course?
--- thinking ---
Thinking Process:

1.  **Analyze the Segment:**
    *   "What is that technology? Or alternatively, do these multiple terms represent or refer to different technologies? In that case, how are they different from each other?" (Raises questions about definitions and differences.)
    *   "One interesting question that may come to your mind along with the previous ones is the significance." (Introduces the concept of importance/relevance.)
    *   "So, I am talking about this term, even this course itself, Foundation of Virtual and Augmented Reality Systems." (Identifies the subject matter.)
    *   "You might be wondering, why so much interest? Why should I learn this course?" (Directly addresses the motivation and relevance of the course.)

2.  **Determine the Main Activity/Goal:**
    *   The lecturer is not defining specific terms yet, nor is he comparing specific technologies in detail.
    *   The primary function is to raise fundamental questions (What is it? How is it different?) and, crucially, to justify *why* the student should care about the subject (significance/interest).
    *   This is a classic introductory move: setting the stage and establishing relevance.

3.  **Apply Constraints & Refine the Goal:**
    *   *Start with a simple verb:* Understand, Explore, Find out.

    *   *Focus on the main activity:* Justifying the study of VR/AR.

    *   *Draft 2:* Explore the differences between technologies and why studying VR/AR matters. (Covers both the questioning and the motivation.)
    *   *Draft 3 (Focusing on the core function):* Find out why Virtual and Augmented Reality systems are important to study. (Very direct and meets all criteria.)

5.  **Final Selection (Choosing Draft 3 for maximum clarity and adherence to the prompt's focus on motivation):** Find out why Virtual and Augmented Reality systems are important to study.
--- answer ---
Find out why Virtual and Augmented Reality systems are important and interesting to learn.

===== S09 subgoal =====
--- prompt ---
Given this segment of a lecture transcript, what goal do you think the lecturer is trying to achieve in this segment? Name the goal starting with a verb. Be fine-grained.

Segment:
Or why should I even be aware of these terms? Or whether these terms have any significance in my future prospects? Why should I know about this? That is, of course, one very natural question that comes to the mind of any learner who starts learning a new thing. Here I am assuming that the majority of you or all of you may not have been exposed to these fields and this question may come naturally to you. In fact, there are many more similar questions that may come to your mind. So, what are we going to do? We are going to learn things and at the end of the learning, at the end of the course, it is expected that you will be able to find answers to these questions.
--- thinking ---
Thinking Process:

The lecturer acknowledges this natural skepticism and then states the course's purpose: "at the end of the course, it is expected that you will be able to find answers to these questions."

2.  **Determine the Main Activity/Goal:** The segment is not defining terms, comparing things, or explaining a specific concept. It is addressing the *relevance* and *purpose* of the entire course/learning process. It is setting expectations for the learner regarding the value of the knowledge.

3.  **Identify the Type of Goal (Preview/Recap/etc.):** This is a motivational/framing statement that sets the overall objective and relevance of the course. It functions as a high-level preview of the *outcome*.

4.  **Drafting the Goal (Applying Constraints):**
    *   *Start with a simple verb:* Understand, Learn, See.
    *   *Be fine-grained and concrete:* The goal is about understanding the *value* and *relevance* of the material.
    *   *Keep it short (max 15 words):*
    *   *Avoid jargon:* (e.g., "contextualize," "paradigm").

5.  **Refining the Draft:**
    *   *Attempt 1:* Understand why learning these terms matters for your future. (A bit vague).
    *   *Attempt 2:* See how the course answers the question of why you should learn this material. (Closer, but slightly awkward).
    *   *Attempt 3 (Focusing on the outcome):* Understand the relevance and value of the course material to your future. (Good, clear, simple).

6.  **Final Selection (Checking constraints):**
    *   Starts with a simple verb (Understand).
    *   Fine-grained (Focuses on relevance/value).
    *   Short (11 words).
    *   Plain language.

7.  **Final Output Generation.**
--- answer ---
Understand the relevance and value of the course material to your future.

These terms will be more precisely defined in subsequent lectures. So, here it is just meant to give you a broader idea, but more precise definitions we will deal with later. And in this course, we will be learning basic concepts, foundational concepts, and practical ways to develop and deploy such systems using one example.

Segment (S14):
So, for some of us, we perceive that 4D, 3D movie theaters where we are watching a documentary on some animals which no longer exist as examples of AR VR. But is that so? In fact, for the majority of us, whenever we use the term VR, probably this image comes to mind: that somebody, some user, wearing something called a headset as shown here. It is called a headset because it is worn on the head and it blocks our vision and we are transported to a virtual world which we can experience through the headset. In fact, this is the most common perception about VR: that somebody is wearing a headset and doing something in that virtual world. Similarly, many of us have played games like Pokemon Go, say for example, where you see the real world on your display screen, device screen, and at certain points. While viewing the real world, you get to see something virtual on the screen which does not exist in the real world. Say, for example, here on this road, this stretch of the road, you get to see a virtual object. Through your screen only, this object does not exist in the real world.
--- thinking ---
Here's a thinking process to arrive at the suggested goal:

1.  **Analyze the Segment (S14):**
    *   The lecturer starts by challenging a common perception (4D/3D theaters as AR/VR examples).
    *   The lecturer then focuses on the *most common perception* of VR: someone wearing a headset, blocked vision, transported to a virtual world.
    *   The lecturer provides a specific example of VR (headset usage).
    *   The lecturer then focuses on the *most common perception* of AR: playing games like Pokémon Go.
    *   The lecturer explains the AR mechanism: seeing the real world on a screen, but seeing virtual objects overlaid on it.

2.  **Determine the Main Activity/Goal:**
    *   The segment is not defining terms (that's S02, S03, S06, etc.).
    *   It is not comparing terms (that's S07, S15, etc.).
    *   It is *illustrating* or *showing* how people commonly experience and perceive VR and AR.
    *   The core action is presenting common, real-world examples of how these technologies are used or imagined.

3.  **Identify Concrete Topics/Examples:**
    *   VR: Headsets, being transported to a virtual world.
    *   AR: Pokémon Go, seeing virtual objects overlaid on the real world via a screen.

4.  **Draft Potential Goals (Focusing on the Learner):**
    *   *Draft 1 (Too academic):* See how common perceptions of VR and AR are formed through examples.
    *   *Draft 2 (Too vague):* See how people experience virtual and augmented reality. (This is close to the original draft goal, but needs to be more specific.)
    *   *Draft 3 (Focusing on the examples):* See how VR is experienced using headsets and AR is seen in games like Pokémon Go.

    *   *Constraint Check:* Name concrete topics (Headsets, Pokémon Go).
    *   *Constraint Check:* Keep it short (Max 15 words).

S38: See the massive growth and market split between hardware and software

S47: Learn how mixed reality was viewed in its early days using the 1994 taxonomy paper

The goal for this segment must be clearly different from the goals of the other segments, especially the ones just before and after it. Name what is specific to this segment (the concrete topics, examples, names or time periods it covers), not what the whole lecture is about.

Write the goal for a learner, so that when they look back at it they immediately remember what was covered.
- First work out what this part of the lecture mainly does, for example: raises questions, looks at everyday examples, defines terms, compares things, explains why something matters, or previews the course. The goal must describe that main activity.
- If the part mainly previews the course or recaps earlier material, the goal is about that preview or recap (e.g. "See what this course covers: its aims, main topics and hands-on parts"), not about the individual topics it lists.
- Do not name topics that are only mentioned in passing.
- Start with a simple verb such as Learn, Understand, See, Compare, Explore or Find out.
- Name the concrete topics, terms or examples covered, e.g. "Learn the differences between viruses and bacteria".
- Use plain, everyday words. Avoid vague or academic words such as delineate, contextualize, establish, foundational, framework, paradigm.
- Keep it short: one line, at most 15 words.

Segment (S15):
So, what is this? Is it AR or VR?
--- thinking ---
Thinking Process:

1.  **Analyze the Request:** The goal is to define the specific learning objective for Segment S15 ("So, what is this? Is it AR or VR?"). The goal must be fine-grained, start with a verb, be learner-focused, short (max 15 words), and clearly distinct from surrounding segments (S14 and S16).

2.  **Analyze S15 Content:** The segment is a rhetorical question: "So, what is this? Is it AR or VR?" This implies the lecturer is presenting an example or scenario and asking the audience to classify it using the terms AR or VR. This is a comparison/application activity.

3.  **Analyze Surrounding Segments:**
    *   S14: See the different ways people experience virtual and augmented reality. (Broad overview of experiences)
    *   S16: See how AR/VR applications go beyond games to show internal structures. (Focus on specific non-game applications)
    *   S13: Understand the difference between VR, AR, MR, and XR. (General definition/difference)
    *   S15 is a specific application of the comparison/difference learned in S13.

4.  **Determine the Main Activity:** The segment is not defining terms (like S02, S03, S10), nor is it giving a broad overview (like S14). It is forcing the learner to *apply* the knowledge of AR vs. VR to a specific, presented example. This is a comparison/classification activity.

5.  **Draft Potential Goals (Focusing on specificity):**
    *   *Draft 1:* Compare an example to determine if it is AR or VR. (A bit generic)
    *   *Draft 2:* Classify a given scenario as either Augmented Reality or Virtual Reality. (More specific to the action)
    *   *Draft 3:* Apply definitions to distinguish between AR and VR technologies. (Uses "apply," which is good)

6.  **Refine and Select the Best Goal (Meeting constraints):** Draft 2 or 3 works well. Since the segment is a direct question about classification, focusing on the comparison/classification aspect is key.

    *   *Final Check:* Starts with a verb? Yes (Classify/Compare). Fine-grained? Yes (AR vs VR classification). Short? Yes. Distinct from S13 (which is general understanding) and S16 (which is application scope)? Yes.

7.  **Final Selection:** Classify a scenario as either Augmented Reality or Virtual Reality. (This captures the essence of the question "So, what is this? Is it AR or VR?")
--- answer ---
Classify a scenario as either Augmented Reality or Virtual Reality.

Segment (S26):
That is a perceived experience through sensory organs which appears to be real. So, every day we are experiencing the real world. Let me use the term 'real' within quotes. When I say immersive, that means we are in an environment, we are experiencing that environment through our sensory organs in such a way that we fail to distinguish between that environment and our real environment. We assume or we perceive that whatever we are experiencing is the real environment. If we are able to do that, that type of technology is immersive technology.

    *   It introduces the term "immersive."
    *   It defines "immersive" as being in an environment experienced through sensory organs such that the user *fails to distinguish* it from the real environment.
    *   The core activity is defining and explaining the concept of immersion.

    *   S26: Defines "immersive technology." (Focus: Specific concept definition)
    *   S27: Understand extended reality as an umbrella term for AR, VR, and MR technologies. (Focus: Umbrella term definition)

4.  **Determine the Main Activity/Goal for S26:** The segment is purely definitional. It explains *what* immersion is, specifically how the sensory experience makes the environment feel real.

    *   *Draft 4 (Combining clarity and specificity):* Understand immersive technology as a sensory experience that feels indistinguishable from reality. (Clear, concise, captures the core idea of "failing to distinguish.")

Segment (S29):
At one extreme of the scale is the real environment, nothing virtual there. At the other extreme is the virtual environment, so there is nothing real here. So, completely virtual at one end, completely real at the other end. In between, there is an interval where at one end we have augmented reality, that means we are augmenting the real world with some digital content, virtual content and at the other end there is augmented virtuality. This is just the opposite of augmented reality where we are augmenting the virtual world with real world content. In augmented reality, we are augmenting the real world with virtual content. In augmented virtuality, we are augmenting the virtual world with real world content. Say for example, here as you can see under augmented virtuality, you can see the human figure which is from the real world augmented within this virtual environment. Whereas, under augmented reality here, what we can see is that digital content, say this character, appearing in the real world setting. So, this in a very broad sense, this entire space between the two extremes, that is virtual environment and real environment, is called mixed reality.

Segment (S39):
There is another thing that aided this growth, of course, as you might all have heard of this term metaverse. Now, this term has stimulated and spurred the growth in the field heavily. Now, there is actually, when I say the term metaverse, it is not merely an application of virtual reality technology or mixed reality technology or augmented reality technology. We can look at it from different points of view. One broad point of view which is likely to spur the growth further is that we are going to reimagine the way humans interact with the digital world. Right now, we are having a very flat type of interactions on flat screens, 2D interactions mostly and occasional 3D interactions and so on. In the Metaverse, we will be acting like we do in the real world. So, it is an all-around 3D world with a 3D environment and there we will be using our so-called quote-unquote natural way of interaction to interact with the digital content. That is a different way of looking at human-computer interaction which opens up many possibilities, many challenges, and we are likely to see further growth if this philosophy takes root, which as of now is taking root. Now, of course, current technology is not yet that far gone. So, there is still significant scope to improve. We can improve the technology and make the overall idea of the metaverse a reality, and companies are investing in that conversion heavily. There are bottlenecks, many technological limitations, but hopefully with more investment, more research, those will be overcome and the growth potential is going to be huge. And the growth potential will be there in both hardware and software. So, what does that point to?

Segment (S45):
Just to recollect, XR refers to the term extended reality. Intuitively, it refers to extending the reality by some means. Systematically, or rather systemically, it refers to using systems to augment our reality or replace our perception of reality rather. So, we have seen that concept of the reality-virtuality continuum, where one end is the complete replacement of reality with virtual reality systems, the other end is the complete replacement of virtuality with the entirely real world; in between there are these extended reality or augmented reality concepts which are broadly referred to as mixed reality. These terms will be more precisely defined in subsequent lectures. So, here it is just meant to give you a broader idea, but more precise definitions we will deal with later. And we have also seen why learning these concepts is important, particularly as these fields are one of the major emerging technology areas recognized by industry, academia, and governments. So, soon we are going to require huge manpower, skilled manpower in this area. So, learning the skills, learning the foundations would certainly be helpful for future opportunities. And in this course, we will be learning basic concepts, foundational concepts, and practical ways to develop and deploy such systems using one example.

===== level 3 grouping (try 1) =====
--- prompt ---
The following are the goals a lecturer pursues, in order, across consecutive parts of one lecture video:

HG2.01: Meet the instructor and explore the fundamental questions of the course
HG2.02: Understand core terms (XR, AR, VR, MR) and the reality-virtuality continuum
HG2.03: Explore the market importance of XR and review the metaverse and course objectives
HG2.04: Compare VR and AR, see applications in heritage, and conclude the lecture

Group these goals into higher-level goals so that they form a hierarchical roadmap of the lecture.
Rules:
- Each group must contain consecutive goals, in the order given.
- Every ID must appear in exactly one group.
- Create between 2 and 2 groups.
- Each higher-level goal must cover everything in its group, while still naming the concrete topics.

How to write each higher-level goal:
Write the goal for a learner, so that when they look back at it they immediately remember what was covered.
- First work out what this part of the lecture mainly does, for example: raises questions, looks at everyday examples, defines terms, compares things, explains why something matters, or previews the course. The goal must describe that main activity.
- If the part mainly previews the course or recaps earlier material, the goal is about that preview or recap (e.g. "See what this course covers: its aims, main topics and hands-on parts"), not about the individual topics it lists.
- Do not name topics that are only mentioned in passing.
- Start with a simple verb such as Learn, Understand, See, Compare, Explore or Find out.
- Name the concrete topics, terms or examples covered, e.g. "Learn the differences between viruses and bacteria".
- Use plain, everyday words. Avoid vague or academic words such as delineate, contextualize, establish, foundational, framework, paradigm.
- Keep it short: one line, at most 15 words.

Answer with only a JSON array, for example:
[{"goal": "Understand ...", "children": ["HG2.01", "HG2.02"]}, {"goal": "Learn ...", "children": ["HG2.03"]}]
--- thinking ---
Here's a thinking process to arrive at the desired JSON output:

1.  **Analyze the Input Goals and Constraints:**
    *   **Goals (in order):**
        *   HG2.01: Meet the instructor and explore the fundamental questions of the course.
        *   HG2.02: Understand core terms (XR, AR, VR, MR) and the reality-virtuality continuum.
        *   HG2.03: Explore the market importance of XR and review the metaverse and course objectives.
        *   HG2.04: Compare VR and AR, see applications in heritage, and conclude the lecture.
    *   **Constraints:**
        *   Groups must contain consecutive goals.
        *   Every ID must be used exactly once.
        *   Create between 2 and 2 groups (meaning exactly 2 groups).
        *   Each higher-level goal must cover everything in its group, naming concrete topics.
        *   *Writing Rules:* Learner-focused, describe the main activity (e.g., Define, Compare, Explore), avoid jargon, keep it short (max 15 words), start with a simple verb.

2.  **Determine Grouping Strategy (2 Groups):**
    *   Since there are 4 goals, the split must be (1+3) or (2+2) or (3+1).
    *   *Option A: (HG2.01) | (HG2.02, HG2.03, HG2.04)*
    *   *Option B: (HG2.01, HG2.02) | (HG2.03, HG2.04)*
    *   *Option C: (HG2.01, HG2.02, HG2.03) | (HG2.04)*

3.  **Evaluate Grouping Options based on Flow and Cohesion:**

    *   **Option A (HG2.01 | HG2.02, HG2.03, HG2.04):**
        *   Group 1 (HG2.01): Introduction/Questions. (Cohesive)
        *   Group 2 (HG2.02, HG2.03, HG2.04): Definitions (XR, AR, VR, MR), Market/Metaverse, Comparison/Applications. (Very broad, but covers the core content.)

    *   **Option B (HG2.01, HG2.02 | HG2.03, HG2.04):**
        *   Group 1 (HG2.01, HG2.02): Introduction + Definitions (XR, AR, VR, MR). (Logical flow: Meet -> Define.)
        *   Group 2 (HG2.03, HG2.04): Market/Metaverse + Comparison/Applications. (Logical flow: Why it matters -> How it works/Examples.)
        *   *This split seems the most balanced and logical.*

    *   **Option C (HG2.01, HG2.02, HG2.03 | HG2.04):**
        *   Group 1 (HG2.01, HG2.02, HG2.03): Intro + Definitions + Market/Metaverse. (A bit heavy.)
        *   Group 2 (HG2.04): Comparison/Applications. (Too small.)

4.  **Select Option B (HG2.01, HG2.02) and (HG2.03, HG2.04) and Draft the Goals:**

    *   **Group 1 (HG2.01, HG2.02):**
        *   *Content:* Meet instructor, fundamental questions, define XR, AR, VR, MR, reality-virtuality continuum.
        *   *Main Activity:* Introduction and defining core concepts.
        *   *Drafting the Goal (Focus on learner benefit, short, simple verb):* Understand the course questions and define core XR terms (XR, AR, VR, MR).
        *   *Refining (Checking constraints):* Simple verb (Understand), names concrete topics (XR terms, questions), short (11 words). Looks good.

    *   **Group 2 (HG2.03, HG2.04):**
        *   *Content:* Market importance of XR, metaverse, course objectives, compare VR/AR, heritage applications.
        *   *Main Activity:* Exploring relevance (market) and practical application/comparison.
        *   *Drafting the Goal (Focus on learner benefit, short, simple verb):* Explore XR's market importance, metaverse, and compare VR/AR applications.
        *   *Refining (Checking constraints):* Simple verb (Explore), names concrete topics (market importance, metaverse, VR/AR applications), short (11 words). Looks good.

5.  **Final JSON Construction:** (Verify structure and constraints one last time.)
    *   2 groups? Yes.
    *   Consecutive goals? Yes.
    *   All IDs used? Yes (HG2.01, HG2.02, HG2.03, HG2.04).
    *   Format: JSON array. (Ready to output.)

What is the overall goal of this lecture video? Name the goal starting with a verb.

Write the goal for a learner, so that when they look back at it they immediately remember what was covered.
- First work out what this part of the lecture mainly does, for example: raises questions, looks at everyday examples, defines terms, compares things, explains why something matters, or previews the course. The goal must describe that main activity.

- Do not name topics that are only mentioned in passing.
- Start with a simple verb such as Learn, Understand, See, Compare, Explore or Find out.
- Name the concrete topics, terms or examples covered, e.g. "Learn the differences between viruses and bacteria".
- Use plain, everyday words. Avoid vague or academic words such as delineate, contextualize, establish, foundational, framework, paradigm.

Reply with only the goal.
--- thinking ---
Here's a thinking process to arrive at the desired goal:

1.  **Analyze the Structure and Content:**
    *   **HG3.01 (Understand the course questions and define core XR terms):** This section is heavily focused on definitions (XR, AR, VR, MR), comparing them, understanding the continuum, and establishing the scope of the field.
    *   **HG3.02 (Explore XR's market importance, metaverse, and compare VR/AR applications):** This section shifts to the *why* (market importance, growth, metaverse) and the *how* (applications, heritage preservation, comparing VR/AR).
    *   **Overall Flow:** The lecture starts by defining the fundamental concepts (What is XR/AR/VR/MR?), moves to the context (Why is this important? Market/Metaverse), and finishes by showing practical applications and concluding the introduction.

2.  **Determine the Main Activity (The "What"):**
    *   Is it just defining terms? No, it also covers market trends and applications.
    *   Is it just previewing the course? While it reviews objectives, the bulk of the content is substantive learning (definitions, market, applications).
    *   The lecture's primary function is to introduce the entire field of Extended Reality, defining its core components, explaining its significance, and showing its real-world use.

3.  **Identify Key Concrete Topics/Terms:**
    *   Core terms: XR, AR, VR, MR.
    *   Concepts: Reality-virtuality continuum, Metaverse.
    *   Context: Market importance, applications (e.g., heritage preservation).

4.  **Draft Potential Goals (Focusing on the Learner's takeaway):**
    *   *Draft 1 (Too academic):* Understand the definitions of XR, AR, VR, and MR and their market implications.
    *   *Draft 2 (Too long):* Learn the core terms (XR, AR, VR, MR), understand the market, and see real-world applications.
    *   *Draft 3 (Focusing on the introduction/scope):* Explore Extended Reality (XR), its core terms, market, and applications.

5.  **Refine and Apply Constraints (Simple verb, short, plain language, concrete):**
    *   The goal must capture the breadth: definitions + importance + use.
    *   *Final Polish:* "Understand XR, AR, and VR: definitions, market importance, and real-world uses." (This is concise, uses simple language, and covers the three major pillars of the lecture.)

6.  **Final Selection:** (Checking against the 15-word limit and tone requirements.) "Understand XR, AR, and VR: definitions, market importance, and real-world uses." (10 words)

Understand XR, AR, and VR: definitions, market importance, and real-world uses.
